Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

 [0,0,3,15,9,1,0,0],
 [0,1,14,15,4,0,0,0],
 [0,0,5,13,11,0,0,0]]
But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

This is not symmetric; maybe actual 8 has loops. Another guess:
[[0,0,6,14,12,1,0,0],
 [0,2,15,14,9,2,0,0],
 [0,2,13,1,13,3,0,0],
 [0,0,1,14,11,1,0,0],
 [0,0,7,15,5,0,0,0],
 [0,0,3,15,9,1,0,0],
 [0,1,14,15,4,0,0,0],
 [0,0,5,13,11,0,0,0]]
But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

[[0,0,6,14,12,1,0,0],
 [0,2,15,14,9,2,0,0],
 [0,2,13,1,13,3,0,0],
 [0,0,1,14,11,1,0,0],

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

 [0,2,13,1,13,3,0,0],

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

 [0,2,15,14,9,2,0,0],
 [0,2,13,1,13,3,0,0],
 [0,0,1,14,11,1,0,0],

But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:

Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. But maybe first is better.

Need consider whether using raw means and nearest centroid is optimal. Perhaps we can improve by centering each image (subtract global mean) and normalizing? Not possible in fixed preprocessing except through weights. But we could use W1 to compute x and 1-x? Actually ReLU can create positive part. We could set W1 to x and maybe -x + threshold to create edges. But then W2 linear over ReLU features could approximate more complex decision. However, manually designing is hard.

Maybe use pairwise comparisons between class templates. We can implement a linear classifier directly with weights equal to differences of class means. For multiclass, score_d = xÂ·mu_d -0.5||mu_d||Â². That's nearest centroid. Fine.

Could include bias term via constant neuron. Since x has no constant, but W1 biases can create constants. But we already handle class-specific biases in b3.

Potential issue: W2 h2 ReLU with b2=20, W3 b3=-20-norm/2. Since h2= xÂ·mu +20. Then output = xÂ·mu -norm/2. Great. No need global mean. But note W2 row has 64 inputs, each entry maybe mu/?? We can use integer template values 0..16 directly, since x normalized 0..1. Then dot is in units of pixel sums, maybe 0-20. Norm/2 = sum(muÂ²)/(512). For mu integers, sum squares maybe 500, norm/2 ~1. So score ~10. Fine.

Would a nearest centroid classifier on raw pixel intensities perform well? Usually yes for digits. Maybe around 85%. Could we use standardized Euclidean with per-pixel variance? We could approximate by scaling each pixel by inverse std. That would improve. We can derive approximate variances from digit shapes. But maybe too much.

Alternative use W1 to compute x_j and x_jÂ²? Not possible because only one linear layer before ReLU; but ReLU can create piecewise linear approximations. Not worth.

Maybe we can leverage 64 first-layer neurons to compute localized features such as each pixel and some sums. Then W2 can use more informative weighted sums. But since W2 is linear, any linear classifier on those features is still linear in x if W1 is linear. Unless W1 uses ReLU to create nonlinear features. We can set W1 rows as x_j - t and -x_j + t, yielding hinge features. Then W2 could learn more complex decision boundaries. But manually deriving weights impossible. However, we could use known feature engineering: binary thresholded pixels. For digit recognition, thresholded images might be more robust. We could set W1 to compute ReLU(x_j - 0.5) (positive ink above threshold) and ReLU(0.5 - x_j) (background). Then W2 class scores based on thresholded patterns. This is still nonlinear and maybe powerful. But designing class weights manually still hard. Yet we could use template matching on thresholded image: score_d = sum over pixels of template_d * ReLU(x_j -0.5) perhaps. This approximates binary shape matching. Could be more robust than intensity. But we'd need W1 64 rows for positive threshold and maybe 64 for negative, total 128 > 64. We only have 64 h1 neurons. Could choose 32 thresholded pixels most discriminative and 32 background? Not enough.

Could use W1 to compute 64 local averages (e.g., 2x2 block sums) then W2 class templates on lower-res 8x8? Not better.

Maybe use W1 as identity and W2 as 10 class templates plus 22 additional "margin" neurons that detect confusions? W3 could combine. Could implement a simple decision tree? Hard.

Let's estimate performance target. We need "useful classifier". Maybe 70% enough? But aim high. Nearest centroid likely 85%. Could possibly reach 90% with good templates. Let's refine templates by averaging multiple examples mentally from group data. Since sample sorted by label, we can approximate means by looking at typical images. I can maybe recall actual mean images from digits dataset more accurately. Let's search memory of sklearn digits mean plot. I think the means are:
